It got noticed at work last week.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck doing these actions, like tapping the scanner or holding your hand still, do you feel a strong urge or discomfort before doing them, almost like you have to do them to feel 'just right' or to relieve some inner tension?\n\nPatient: Not really like an urge, no. Last week they wrote me up for it.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those repetitive actions at work, like tapping the scanner, do you feel like you have to do them in a certain way to prevent something bad from happening, or do they feel more like an urge that you can't control?\n\nPatient: It’s not really like I’m trying to stop something bad, no. It’s more… I just get stuck in it. Like the scanner tapping has a pattern, or my hand just stays over the drawer even though the customer’s right there. I know it doesn’t fit what’s happening, but I don’t move on fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, brief movements or sounds that you couldn’t control, like blinking, shrugging, or making a noise?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like sudden noises or blinking I can’t control.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had unwanted, intrusive thoughts that cause you distress, or found yourself doing mental acts—like counting or repeating words silently—to try to reduce that distress or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I get stuck sometimes, but not because I’m counting in my head or trying to stop something from happening. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any uncontrollable movements or gestures, like sudden jerks, facial twitches, or unusual postures, that you couldn’t stop yourself from doing?\n\nPatient: Not like jerks or twitches, no. It’s more that I get stuck doing something that doesn’t fit… like tapping the scanner in a pattern, or just holding my hand over the cash drawer while someone’s waiting. I don’t really mean to. It got noticed at work last week.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck doing these actions, like tapping the scanner or holding your hand still, do you feel a strong urge or discomfort before doing them, almost like you have to do them to feel 'just right' or to relieve some inner tension?\n\nPatient: Not really like an urge, no. I don’t think I’m trying to make it feel “right.” I just… get stuck doing it. Like tapping the scanner in a pattern or holding my hand over the drawer while the person’s waiting. That’s the stuff that got me in trouble last week.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong inner need or pressure to move in a certain way, like a feeling that builds up and you have to do the movement to release it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t really feel it building up like that. It’s more like I just get stuck doing something weird at the register—tapping the scanner in a pattern, or freezing with my hand over the cash drawer while someone’s waiting. It got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself stuck doing things like tapping the scanner or holding your hand still, are you fully aware of what you're doing at the time, or does it feel like you're spacing out or not completely in control of your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m aware enough to know it looks wrong. I’m there at the register, and then I’m tapping the scanner in a pattern or just… stopped with my hand over the cash drawer while they’re waiting. It’s not like I plan it. It just happens, and last week they wrote me up for it.\n\nDoctor: Before you start tapping the scanner or holding your hand still, do you ever feel a strange sensation in your body or a need to do it that comes on suddenly, like an itch or pressure that you have to follow through with?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really notice an itch or pressure first. It’s more just… I’m at the register and I end up tapping the scanner, or I freeze with my hand over the drawer while the customer’s waiting. That’s what got me written up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
